What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I’ve been having trouble staying seated when I’m supposed to. Like during client interviews, tutoring sessions, even book club. I keep getting up, and people are starting to notice that I can’t just sit and listen.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're seated?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Mostly when I’m stuck at the computer going through records. My foot’s usually bouncing, or I’m clicking a pen, shifting around in the chair. Sometimes I move so much I lose my place in what I’m reading.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or stressed when you're fidgeting or feeling restless?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it anxious. Sometimes I’m just… uncomfortable sitting still. I haven’t really paid attention to whether it’s stress or not.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeating certain actions or movements over and over?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so. When I’m at the computer with records, I’ll keep bouncing my foot, clicking a pen, shifting around in the chair. Sometimes it’s enough that I lose where I was reading.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these repetitive movements?\n\nPatient: Not like I have to, exactly. It’s more like… if I’m stuck at the computer going through records, my foot just starts bouncing, or I’m clicking a pen, shifting around. I don’t really think about it until I realize I’ve lost my place reading.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like bouncing your foot or clicking a pen helps you feel better when you're stuck at the computer?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little. It’s not like I think, “this will help,” but when I’m stuck combing through records, my foot starts bouncing or I click a pen. I guess it takes the edge off sitting there, until I realize I’ve shifted around so much I’ve lost my place.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these movements or actions are something you can't control?\n\nPatient: I can control it if I notice it, I guess. It’s just… most of the time I don’t notice until my foot’s been bouncing under the desk or I’ve been clicking the pen long enough that I’ve lost my place in what I’m reading.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with sitting still and fidgeting causing problems for you in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I don’t love admitting that, but it’s starting to affect my work. I’ve missed details in investigations, and some clients get irritated with me. A couple of the junior detectives seem less willing to debrief with me now, like they feel I’m steamrolling them.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel when you're unable to sit still or control your fidgeting?\n\nPatient: It’s… frustrating more than anything. Not unbearable, but enough that it gets under my skin. Like I should be able to just sit there and get through it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these movements are habits that you've developed over time?\n\nPatient: I suppose so, yeah. It’s not like I decided to do it, but when I’m stuck at the computer going through records, my foot starts bouncing, or I’m clicking a pen, shifting around… and then I realize I’ve lost my place. So maybe it’s become a habit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these movements because you want to or because you feel like you have to?\n\nPatient: It’s not really because I want to. It’s more like… I don’t notice it starting. I’ll be at the computer going through records, and my foot’s bouncing or I’m clicking a pen, shifting around. By the time I catch it, I’ve already lost my place.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to achieve something specific by bouncing your foot or clicking a pen, like relieving anxiety or preventing something from happening?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I’m not trying to prevent anything or… calm myself down exactly. It just happens when I’m stuck reading records on the computer. My foot starts bouncing, or I’m clicking a pen, shifting around, and then I realize I’ve lost my place.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these movements in response to any specific thoughts or urges?\n\nPatient: Not really specific thoughts, no. It’s more just when I’m sitting there too long, combing through records on the computer. Mostly when I’m stuck at the computer going through records. My foot’s usually bouncing, or I’m clicking a pen, shifting around in the chair. Sometimes it’s enough that I lose where I was reading.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these repetitive movements?\n\nPatient: Not like I have to, exactly. It’s more like… if I’m stuck at the computer going through records, my foot just starts bouncing, or I’m clicking a pen, shifting around. I don’t really think about it until I realize I’ve lost my place reading.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like bouncing your foot or clicking a pen helps you feel better when you're stuck at the computer?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little. It’s not like I think, “this will help,” but when I’m stuck combing through records, my foot starts bouncing or I click a pen. I guess it takes the edge off sitting there, until I realize I’ve shifted around so much I’ve lost my place.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these movements or actions are something you can't control?\n\nPatient: I can control it if I notice it, I guess. It’s just… most of the time I don’t notice until my foot’s been bouncing under the desk or I’ve been clicking the pen long enough that I’ve lost my place in what I’m reading.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with sitting still and fidgeting causing problems for you in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. So maybe it’s become a habit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these movements because you want to or because you feel like you have to?\n\nPatient: It’s not really because I want to. It’s more like… I don’t notice it starting. I’ll be at the computer going through records, and my foot’s bouncing or I’m clicking a pen, shifting around. By the time I catch it, I’ve already lost my place.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to achieve something specific by bouncing your foot or clicking a pen, like relieving anxiety or preventing something from happening?\n\nPatient: No, not really. So maybe it’s become a habit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these movements because you want to or because you feel like you have to?\n\nPatient: It’s not really because I want to. It’s more like… I don’t notice it starting. I’ll be at the computer going through records, and my foot’s bouncing or I’m clicking a pen, shifting around. By the time I catch it, I’ve already lost my place.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to achieve something specific by bouncing your foot or clicking a pen, like relieving anxiety or preventing something from happening?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I’m not trying to prevent anything or… calm myself down exactly. It just happens when I’m stuck reading records on the computer. My foot starts bouncing, or I’m clicking a pen, shifting around, and then I realize I’ve lost my place.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these movements in response to any specific thoughts or urges?\n\nPatient: Not really specific thoughts, no. A couple of the junior detectives seem less willing to debrief with me now, like they feel I’m steamrolling them.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel when you're unable to sit still or control your fidgeting?\n\nPatient: It’s… frustrating more than anything. Not unbearable, but enough that it gets under my skin. When I’m at the computer going through records and I catch myself bouncing my foot or clicking a pen, or shifting around so much I lose my place, I feel irritated with myself. Like I should be able to just sit there and get through it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these movements are habits that you've developed over time?\n\nPatient: I suppose so, yeah. It’s not like I decided to do it, but when I’m stuck at the computer going through records, my foot starts bouncing, or I’m clicking a pen, shifting around… and then I realize I’ve lost my place. So maybe it’s become a habit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these movements because you want to or because you feel like you have to?\n\nPatient: It’s not really because I want to. It’s more like… I don’t notice it starting. I’ll be at the computer going through records, and my foot’s bouncing or I’m clicking a pen, shifting around. By the time I catch it, I’ve already lost my place.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to achieve something specific by bouncing your foot or clicking a pen, like relieving anxiety or preventing something from happening?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I’m not trying to prevent anything or… calm myself down exactly. It just happens when I’m stuck reading records on the computer. My foot starts bouncing, or I’m clicking a pen, shifting around, and then I realize I’ve lost my place.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these movements in response to any specific thoughts or urges?\n\nPatient: Not really specific thoughts, no. It’s more just when I’m sitting there too long, combing through records on the computer. I’ll realize my foot’s going under the desk, or I’m clicking a pen, shifting around… and then I’ve lost my place.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these movements to make yourself feel better or to prevent something from happening?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it’s to prevent anything. It’s just when I’m stuck at the computer going through records, my foot’s bouncing or I’m clicking a pen, shifting around, and then I lose my place.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you could stop bouncing your foot or clicking a pen if you really wanted to?\n\nPatient: I mean, probably for a little bit if I really focused on it. But when I’m stuck at the computer going through records, it just starts up again—my foot bouncing, clicking the pen, shifting around—and then I notice I’ve lost my place.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your difficulty sitting still and fidgeting is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I wouldn’t say it’s “distress” exactly, but it’s becoming a problem. Professionally, I mean. I’ve missed details in cases, and clients get irritated. Some of the junior detectives don’t really want to debrief with me because they feel like I’m… steamrolling them. So, yes, it’s costing me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F98.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I’ve been having trouble staying seated when I’m supposed to. Like during client interviews, tutoring sessions, even book club. I keep getting up, and people are starting to notice that I can’t just sit and listen.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're seated?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Mostly when I’m stuck at the computer going through records. My foot’s usually bouncing, or I’m clicking a pen, shifting around in the chair. Sometimes I move so much I lose my place in what I’m reading.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or stressed when you're fidgeting or feeling restless?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it anxious. Sometimes I’m just… uncomfortable sitting still. I haven’t really paid attention to whether it’s stress or not.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeating certain actions or movements over and over?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so. When I’m at the computer with records, I’ll keep bouncing my foot, clicking a pen, shifting around in the chair. Sometimes it’s enough that I lose where I was reading.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these repetitive movements?\n\nPatient: Not like I have to, exactly. It’s more like… if I’m stuck at the computer going through records, my foot just starts bouncing, or I’m clicking a pen, shifting around. I don’t really think about it until I realize I’ve lost my place reading.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like bouncing your foot or clicking a pen helps you feel better when you're stuck at the computer?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little. It’s not like I think, “this will help,” but when I’m stuck combing through records, my foot starts bouncing or I click a pen. I guess it takes the edge off sitting there, until I realize I’ve shifted around so much I’ve lost my place.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these movements or actions are something you can't control?\n\nPatient: I can control it if I notice it, I guess. It’s just… most of the time I don’t notice until my foot’s been bouncing under the desk or I’ve been clicking the pen long enough that I’ve lost my place in what I’m reading.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with sitting still and fidgeting causing problems for you in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I don’t love admitting that, but it’s starting to affect my work. I’ve missed details in investigations, and some clients get irritated with me. A couple of the junior detectives seem less willing to debrief with me now, like they feel I’m steamrolling them.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel when you're unable to sit still or control your fidgeting?\n\nPatient: It’s… frustrating more than anything. Not unbearable, but enough that it gets under my skin. When I’m at the computer going through records and I catch myself bouncing my foot or clicking a pen, or shifting around so much I lose my place, I feel irritated with myself. Like I should be able to just sit there and get through it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these movements are habits that you've developed over time?\n\nPatient: I suppose so, yeah. It’s not like I decided to do it, but when I’m stuck at the computer going through records, my foot starts bouncing, or I’m clicking a pen, shifting around… and then I realize I’ve lost my place. So maybe it’s become a habit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these movements because you want to or because you feel like you have to?\n\nPatient: It’s not really because I want to. It’s more like… I don’t notice it starting. I’ll be at the computer going through records, and my foot’s bouncing or I’m clicking a pen, shifting around. By the time I catch it, I’ve already lost my place.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to achieve something specific by bouncing your foot or clicking a pen, like relieving anxiety or preventing something from happening?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I’m not trying to prevent anything or… calm myself down exactly. It just happens when I’m stuck reading records on the computer. My foot starts bouncing, or I’m clicking a pen, shifting around, and then I realize I’ve lost my place.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these movements in response to any specific thoughts or urges?\n\nPatient: Not really specific thoughts, no. It’s more just when I’m sitting there too long, combing through records on the computer. I’ll realize my foot’s going under the desk, or I’m clicking a pen, shifting around… and then I’ve lost my place.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these movements to make yourself feel better or to prevent something from happening?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it’s to prevent anything. It’s just when I’m stuck at the computer going through records, my foot’s bouncing or I’m clicking a pen, shifting around, and then I lose my place.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you could stop bouncing your foot or clicking a pen if you really wanted to?\n\nPatient: I mean, probably for a little bit if I really focused on it. But when I’m stuck at the computer going through records, it just starts up again—my foot bouncing, clicking the pen, shifting around—and then I notice I’ve lost my place.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your difficulty sitting still and fidgeting is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I wouldn’t say it’s “distress” exactly, but it’s becoming a problem. Professionally, I mean. I’ve missed details in cases, and clients get irritated. Some of the junior detectives don’t really want to debrief with me because they feel like I’m… steamrolling them. So, yes, it’s costing me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these movements because they're something you're used to doing, or because something would feel off if you didn't do them?\n\nPatient: More like I’m used to doing it, I guess. If I’m combing through records on the computer, my foot starts bouncing, or I’m clicking a pen, shifting in the chair… and then I realize I’ve lost my place. It’s not like something bad happens if I don’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like something would be wrong or unfinished if you didn't bounce your foot or click your pen when you're sitting at the computer?\n\nPatient: No… not wrong or unfinished, exactly. It’s just when I’m at the computer going through records, my foot starts bouncing or I’m clicking a pen, shifting around. Then I realize I’ve lost my place in what I was reading.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yeah… I think I would. Not like intense anxiety, but I get kind of tense or restless if I have to sit totally still. At the computer, especially, my foot starts bouncing under the desk or I’m clicking a pen, shifting around… and then I lose my place in what I’m reading.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F98.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I’ve been having trouble staying seated when I’m supposed to. Like during client interviews, tutoring sessions, even book club. I keep getting up, and people are starting to notice that I can’t just sit and listen.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're seated?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Mostly when I’m stuck at the computer going through records. My foot’s usually bouncing, or I’m clicking a pen, shifting around in the chair. Sometimes I move so much I lose my place in what I’m reading.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or stressed when you're fidgeting or feeling restless?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it anxious. Sometimes I’m just… uncomfortable sitting still. I haven’t really paid attention to whether it’s stress or not.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeating certain actions or movements over and over?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so. When I’m at the computer with records, I’ll keep bouncing my foot, clicking a pen, shifting around in the chair. Sometimes it’s enough that I lose where I was reading.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these repetitive movements?\n\nPatient: Not like I have to, exactly. It’s more like… if I’m stuck at the computer going through records, my foot just starts bouncing, or I’m clicking a pen, shifting around. I don’t really think about it until I realize I’ve lost my place reading.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like bouncing your foot or clicking a pen helps you feel better when you're stuck at the computer?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little. It’s not like I think, “this will help,” but when I’m stuck combing through records, my foot starts bouncing or I click a pen. I guess it takes the edge off sitting there, until I realize I’ve shifted around so much I’ve lost my place.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these movements or actions are something you can't control?\n\nPatient: I can control it if I notice it, I guess. It’s just… most of the time I don’t notice until my foot’s been bouncing under the desk or I’ve been clicking the pen long enough that I’ve lost my place in what I’m reading.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with sitting still and fidgeting causing problems for you in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I don’t love admitting that, but it’s starting to affect my work. I’ve missed details in investigations, and some clients get irritated with me. A couple of the junior detectives seem less willing to debrief with me now, like they feel I’m steamrolling them.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel when you're unable to sit still or control your fidgeting?\n\nPatient: It’s… frustrating more than anything. Not unbearable, but enough that it gets under my skin. When I’m at the computer going through records and I catch myself bouncing my foot or clicking a pen, or shifting around so much I lose my place, I feel irritated with myself. Like I should be able to just sit there and get through it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these movements are habits that you've developed over time?\n\nPatient: I suppose so, yeah. It’s not like I decided to do it, but when I’m stuck at the computer going through records, my foot starts bouncing, or I’m clicking a pen, shifting around… and then I realize I’ve lost my place. So maybe it’s become a habit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these movements because you want to or because you feel like you have to?\n\nPatient: It’s not really because I want to. It’s more like… I don’t notice it starting. I’ll be at the computer going through records, and my foot’s bouncing or I’m clicking a pen, shifting around. By the time I catch it, I’ve already lost my place.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to achieve something specific by bouncing your foot or clicking a pen, like relieving anxiety or preventing something from happening?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I’m not trying to prevent anything or… calm myself down exactly. It just happens when I’m stuck reading records on the computer. My foot starts bouncing, or I’m clicking a pen, shifting around, and then I realize I’ve lost my place.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these movements in response to any specific thoughts or urges?\n\nPatient: Not really specific thoughts, no. It’s more just when I’m sitting there too long, combing through records on the computer. I’ll realize my foot’s going under the desk, or I’m clicking a pen, shifting around… and then I’ve lost my place.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these movements to make yourself feel better or to prevent something from happening?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it’s to prevent anything. It’s just when I’m stuck at the computer going through records, my foot’s bouncing or I’m clicking a pen, shifting around, and then I lose my place.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you could stop bouncing your foot or clicking a pen if you really wanted to?\n\nPatient: I mean, probably for a little bit if I really focused on it. But when I’m stuck at the computer going through records, it just starts up again—my foot bouncing, clicking the pen, shifting around—and then I notice I’ve lost my place.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your difficulty sitting still and fidgeting is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I wouldn’t say it’s “distress” exactly, but it’s becoming a problem. Professionally, I mean. I’ve missed details in cases, and clients get irritated. Some of the junior detectives don’t really want to debrief with me because they feel like I’m… steamrolling them. So, yes, it’s costing me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these movements because they're something you're used to doing, or because something would feel off if you didn't do them?\n\nPatient: More like I’m used to doing it, I guess. If I’m combing through records on the computer, my foot starts bouncing, or I’m clicking a pen, shifting in the chair… and then I realize I’ve lost my place. It’s not like something bad happens if I don’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like something would be wrong or unfinished if you didn't bounce your foot or click your pen when you're sitting at the computer?\n\nPatient: No… not wrong or unfinished, exactly. It’s just when I’m at the computer going through records, my foot starts bouncing or I’m clicking a pen, shifting around. Then I realize I’ve lost my place in what I was reading.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would experience anxiety or discomfort if you were prevented from bouncing your foot or clicking your pen while sitting at the computer?\n\nPatient: Not really anxiety, no. It’s more that when I’m stuck there combing through records, my foot starts going, or I’m clicking the pen, shifting around… and then I’ve lost my place. If someone stopped me, I’d probably feel a little irritated, but not panicked or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would feel uncomfortable or tense if you tried to sit completely still for an extended period of time without fidgeting?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would. Not like intense anxiety, but I get kind of tense or restless if I have to sit totally still. At the computer, especially, my foot starts bouncing under the desk or I’m clicking a pen, shifting around… and then I lose my place in what I’m reading.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
